Text:"2026-10-20 Deep Fusion Memory: Chunk-Level Parametric Memory for Large Language Models Rubin Wei1,2, Siyuan Sun1, Hao Sun1,2, Yuliang Liu1,2, Xiang Shi1, Jiarui Wang1,2, Junming Zhang1,2, Zhouhan Lin1,2 1 LUMIA Lab, School of Artificial Intelligence, Shanghai Jiao Tong University 2 Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory # weirubinn@gmail.com, lin.zhouhan@gmail.com Corresponding Author. Abstract Large language models need effective ways to acquire and use external knowledge beyond what they retain from pretraining. Continued pretraining risks catastrophic forgetting, while retrieval at inference time can add substantial overhead. External parametric memory avoids online retrieval, but constructing its training data requires extensive offline search, and output-level interpolation provides only shallow fusion. We introduce Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), parametric memory architecture that reduces this construction workload and incorporates learned memory into intermediate hidden states. causal memory predictor learns to generate memory from the preceding context, using retrieved chunk embeddings from the corpus as supervision. The predicted memory is shared across tokens within each chunk and fused into their hidden states through gated cross-attention. Memory prediction and fusion are trained end to end with the backbone frozen, and inference requires no explicit retrieval. Compared with existing parametric memory approaches, which often construct token-level memory targets, DFM requires 32 fewer retrieval queries and 2048 fewer retrieved items to construct its training data. Across six QA tasks, DFM performs better over the strongest baseline by 12.71% on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and 11.19% on Qwen3-8B-Base. Beyond QA, domain adaptation yields average gains of 6.51 and 4.38 points over Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively. The code will be released soon. 1. Introduction Large language models (LLMs) (Yang et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025; Team et al., 2025; Bai et al.,"

Text:"Toward Recursive Self-Improvement in Physical AI Meng Luo1 Zechen Bai1 Shanqing Xu2 Yanlin Li1 Kaixin Li1 Mengchen Qian2 Weifeng Lu3 Ran Zhang4 Yingtian Zou3 Ziyang Luo5 Wei Chen2 Hongzhan Lin1* 2Huazhong University of Science and Technology 1National University of Singapore 3Shanghai Jiao Tong University 4Nanyang Technological University 5Hong Kong Baptist University Recursive self-improvement (RSI) describes system that improves not only its behavior but also the process by which it improves. Recent digital agents test this idea by rewriting their own code and checking each version with executable tests. Bringing these mechanisms into the physical world requires learning from partial observations through costly trials, while accounting for changing operating conditions and potentially irreversible consequences. We position RSI within robot learning and trace pathways from improving robot capabilities to improving the processes that generate them. We examine how physical experience can strengthen these improvement mechanisms and when the resulting gains can support further self-improvement of the mechanisms themselves. Gains carry forward only when feedback addresses remaining learning needs, the receiving component can use the change, its value survives changes in tasks or hardware, and continued practice remains safe and affordable. Existing studies, concentrated in manipulation, support behavioral improvement, benefits to later learning, and procedure improvement. Some systems also change the improvers knowledge or tools, but the physical studies reviewed here have yet to isolate gains attributable to recursion. We synthesize these findings into evaluation principles and research directions for machines that, trial by trial, safely improve the way they improve. https://physical2rsi.github.io (cid:128) Project GitHub https://github.com/Physical2RSI/Awesome-Physical2RSI # Contact mluo@u.nus.edu, danielhzlin@nus.edu.sg Digital RSI supplies the mechanisms (cid:29) P"

Text:"Verus-Insight Verus-Insight: Towards Explainable Artifact Detection in AI-Generated Videos Zhuoran Zhang1,2,*, Yang Shi1,2,*,, Qixun Wang1,2,*, Tengfei Liu1, Yuqi Tang3, Xuanyu Zhu1, Yizhuo Jia4, Jingyun Hua2, Shixuan Liu5,, Xianghua Ying1,, Haotian Wang5,, Yuanxing Zhang2,, Pengfei Wan2, Wenjing Yang 1Peking University 2Kling Team 3HKUST (GZ) 5Tsinghua University 4Fudan University Equal Contribution Corresponding Author Project Lead (cid:209) Homepage Code (cid:242) Model ı Dataset Abstract Recent advances in video generation have made synthetic videos increasingly realistic, placing greater demands on reliable AI-generated video detection. Existing artifact-based detectors typically identify generation artifacts as evidence for source prediction, yet they may either fail to recognize subtle artifacts or reach the correct source decision without faithfully identifying the evidence underlying it. In this work, we systematically investigate how to design and train effective artifact detectors and formulate artifact detection as structured process comprising three complementary capabilities: Inspection Planning, which determines what should be examined; Evidence Verification, which verifies whether the inspected content exhibits genuine generation artifacts; and Evidence Aggregation, which integrates the verified evidence into the final source decision. Building on this formulation, we introduce Verus-Insight, trained with human-anchored full-process supervision and progressive capability training to strengthen both fine-grained artifact detection and video-level detection. We further introduce Verus-Bench, benchmark with fine-grained human annotations of perceptible artifacts for evaluating artifact detection. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that Verus-Insight achieves strong performance in both fine-grained artifact analysis and AI-generated video detection, with substantial gains in recovering human-perceived artifacts. Further analyses "

Naive RAG Is Stronger Than We Thought Zhengye Bao1 Shengtao Zhang2,1,* Haokai Zhang3,1,* Shiqi Lou4 Muning Wen2, Minnan Luo1, https://github.com/Litmeb/RAW-RAG LLM memory is widely used in long-term conversation understanding. Agentic memory systems often use mutation, structural organization, learned retrieval strategies, or other complex operations. Is this complexity always necessary? We study RAW-RAG, naive RAG baseline using dense retrieval and reranking over raw history to supply evidence to reader LLM. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval, MEME, and MemoryAgentBench, RAWRAG ranks first among the compared memory systems in six of seven benchmarkmodel settings, outperforming the strongest compared agentic memory system by 0.135.53 points. We investigate why raw-history retrieval remains competitive. Evidence analysis shows that its higher coverage of necessary evidence can offset lower utilization. Useful evidence includes both answer values and the relations and dependency paths needed to derive them. Even complete access to transformed memory stores leaves an accuracy gap relative to raw evidence, highlighting the importance of preserving raw information. These findings challenge the necessity of complex agentic memory for the evaluated conversation tasks and establish naive RAG as an essential baseline. The AgentVQA Benchmark. AgentVQA unifies five challenging agentic domains: Web Agents, Egocentric Videos, Robotics, Games, and Spatial Planning. Abstract Vision-language models (VLMs) can perform broad range of tasks across diverse settings, yet their performance in agentic contexts remains poorly understood. Although online evaluations in simulators and real-world environments are essential for measuring interactive agent performance, they are costly and challenging to scale. Complementary offline VQA benchmarks can enable faster and more reliable comparisons across agentic domains, yet existing benchmarks remain limited and fragmented. To address this gap, we introduce AgentVQA, an offline benchmark for evaluating VLMs on realistic agentic scenarios across five agent domains: Web Agents, Robotics, Egocentric Videos, Games, and Spatial Planning. We build AgentVQA by manually selecting 1000 challenging, real-world agent examples from 23 source datasets and adapting them for reliable offline evaluation. The resulting benchmark preserves realistic scenarios from interactive environments across diverse domains, and its model rankings track those from corresponding online evaluations. Across the 22 evaluated models, the best achieves only 58.5% accuracy. Our cross-domain analysis shows that errors are driven primarily by failures to ground and maintain accurate representations of the current and evolving visual state, including localizing relevant UI elements, interpreting motion, tracking state changes over time, and inferring 3D spatial relationships, rather than by task misinterpretation or downstream logical reasoning. *Equal advising. Text:"Reading, Writing, Sharing, and Interacting with the World through Video Survey Toward Unified Video World Models Shengqiong Wu1,, Meng Luo2,, Bobo Li2, Ziwei Liu3, Shuicheng Yan2, Ming-Hsuan Yang4, Philip Torr1, Hao Fei1, 1University of Oxford 2NUS 3NTU 4University of California, Merced Project lead, correspondence (haofei7419@gmail.com) Equal contribution Project: https://vid-gen.github.io/video-world-model-survey GitHub: https://github.com/vid-gen/awesome-video-world-model Abstract Video foundation models have advanced rapidly in understanding and generating visual sequences, while video world models seek to capture the underlying dynamics of the world they depict. Despite their different objectives, both concern the relationship between video observations and an evolving world. This survey adopts world modeling as unifying lens for examining these developments. Viewing an observed video as partial, lossy, and temporally ordered projection of latent temporal world, we organize the literature around four complementary operations: reading infers world properties from observed video; (cid:210) writing synthesizes video from specified or learned world conditions; sharing integrates understanding and generation through common modeling mechanisms; and (cid:132) interacting predicts and updates world states under actions and evolving observations. This perspective distinguishes temporal coherence in observed or generated sequences from consistency in the underlying world state. Across tasks, representations, models, datasets, and benchmarks, we trace shift in emphasis from clip-level recognition and perceptual fidelity toward persistent state, controllable evolution, physical and causal validity, and evaluation grounded in interaction and downstream decisions, and we distill the open challenges that follow. We present AnyGameRenderer, video-to-video generative renderer that turns such footage into high-quality visuals with characters, motion detail, and effects. Throughout, it keeps the layout, camera, and every entitys position and timing intact. Our AnyGameRenderer generalizes across wide range of genres that agents build, such as tower defense, auto-battlers, top-down survivors, turn-based battles, kart racing, and crowd runners. AnyGameRenderer takes plain video as its game-state input, with no complex representations such as engine G-buffers, per-object class labels, or low-poly proxies. Gray blockouts, colored primitives, semantic maps, and wireframes all work as input. It also preserves entity correspondence: group counts stay correct, and entities appear and disappear on the right frame. We implement AnyGameRenderer by LoRA fine-tuning MiniMax-H3 ref2va, 33B reference-video-conditioned video generation model. Training uses 10k paired clips from three sources: (i) multi-stream rendering data from 38 agent-made games exported from GEAR, an AI game-creation tool; (ii) two-stream rendering data from Unreal Engine, with programmatically controlled crowds, count changes, and entity scales; and (iii) real gameplay recordings, with pairs reconstructed via 3D-box detection. To evaluate the entity correspondence, we propose an entitylevel benchmark of 59 held-out cases with 753 entities, scored against ground-truth boxes. Abstract OpenDS-World introduces a live-web benchmark to evaluate autonomous multi-step deep search agents, revealing significant gaps between frontier models and human performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Despite substantial progress in Deep Search agents, their ability to conduct end-to-end research in real-world open Web environments remains insufficiently evaluated. Unlike static retrieval settings, information on the real-world Web is distributed across diverse sources, modalities, and heterogeneous formats, often involving complex interaction mechanisms that require agents to autonomously search for, access, and integrate relevant evidence during execution. To address this gap, we introduce the OpenDS task, designed to evaluate agents' ability to perform multi-step deep search in the live, open Web. This task requires agents to autonomously explore the real Web, discover and connect relevant evidence from multiple independent sources through Web search, browser interaction, and evidence processing, and ultimately determine a unique factual answer. Building on this task formulation, we construct OpenDS-World, a benchmark comprising 200 challenging tasks spanning diverse information modalities, heterogeneous file formats, and interactive access requirements, with independent human verification to ensure evidence validity and answer uniqueness. We evaluate 12 frontier models under a unified tool environment and execution protocol. The best-performing model achieves 39.0% accuracy, while human evaluation across 1,000 independent attempts yields 21.1% accuracy. Further analyses of tool usage, search cost, termination behavior, and failure recovery reveal substantial differences in research strategies across models, as well as persistent challenges in tool coordination and long-horizon search management. OpenDS-World provides a unified evaluation platform for systematically assessing and advancing agents' autonomous deep search capabilities in real-world open Web environments.

Задача OpenDS требует от агента самостоятельно искать, открывать и объединять информацию из разных источников, модальностей и форматов файлов, а затем находить единственно верный фактический ответ. Abstract Factor-Aware Predictive Learning enables video diffusion transformers to leverage multimodal language model representations for sparse temporal inference and factor-aware cinematic control through staged grounding and optimization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Cinematic video generation requires both temporal inference from limited inputs (i.e., sparse rather than frame-by-frame guidance) and factor-aware control of character, scene, action, and camera. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offer useful priors for both capabilities, but their representations are not automatically actionable for video generation: a Video Diffusion Transformer (DiT) may bypass them through native conditioning pathways, and using them does not ensure either factor-aware control or generation-compatible temporal prediction. We propose Factor-Aware Predictive Learning (FAPL), which establishes effective MLLM latent use as a shared prerequisite and then targets the two capabilities with complementary learning signals. First, latent-only generator grounding trains the DiT with a frozen MLLM and connector, strengthening the DiT's use of MLLM latents for generation. Second, for factor-aware control, paired videos specify a target-factor change and non-target preservation, and factor-span replacement trains the DiT to realize this relationship in its output. Third, for temporal inference, text-only and text-plus-complete-video observations share a video target, and the trained DiT is then frozen while the MLLM and connector are optimized through it. This final stage trains limited-input representations for generation while retaining complete-observation reconstruction as a complementary training mode. Extensive experiments demonstrate our consistently better performance on three cinematic video generation tasks (i.e., sparse-to-video, factor-aware editing, and multi-shot generation) over baselines and validate FAPL's contributions to MLLM feature use, temporal control, and factor coordination.
[23.10.2026 06:54] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен метод Factor-Aware Predictive Learning (FAPL), который позволяет Video Diffusion Transformer (DiT) эффективно использовать латентные представления Multimodal LLM для генерации кинематографического видео. Обучение проходит в три этапа: сначала DiT учится опираться на латенты замороженной MLLM, затем — контролировать отдельные факторы (персонаж, сцена, действие, камера) с помощью пар видео и замены фрагментов, и наконец — выполнять временной вывод по разреженным входным данным. Эксперименты показывают превосходство FAPL над базовыми методами в задачах sparse-to-video, редактирования с учетом факторов и многокадровой генерации. Исследование подтверждает вклад каждого этапа в использование признаков MLLM, временной контроль и координацию факторов.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Управляй кадром через MLLM: от разреженных подсказок к кинематографическому видео"}

"Abstract Factor-Aware Predictive Learning enables video diffusion transformers to leverage multimodal language model representations for sparse temporal inference and factor-aware cinematic control through staged grounding and optimization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Cinematic video generation requires both temporal inference from limited inputs (i.e., sparse rather than frame-by-frame guidance) and factor-aware control of character, scene, action, and camera. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offer useful priors for both capabilities, but their representations are not automatically actionable for video generation: a Video Diffusion Transformer (DiT) may bypass them through native conditioning pathways, and using them does not ensure either factor-aware control or generation-compatible temporal prediction. We propose Factor-Aware Predictive Learning (FAPL), which establishes effective MLLM latent use as a shared prerequisite and then targets the two capabilities with complementary learning signals. First, latent-only generator grounding trains the DiT with a frozen MLLM and connector, strengthening the DiT's use of MLLM latents for generation. Second, for factor-aware control, paired videos specify a target-factor change and non-target preservation, and factor-span replacement trains the DiT to realize this relationship in its output. Third, for temporal inference, text-only and text-plus-complete-video observations share a video target, and the trained DiT is then frozen while the MLLM and connector are optimized through it. This final stage trains limited-input representations for generation while retaining complete-observation reconstruction as a complementary training mode. Extensive experiments demonstrate our consistently better performance on three cinematic video generation tasks (i.e., sparse-to-video, factor-aware editing, and multi-shot generation) over baselines and validate FAPL's contributions to MLLM feature use, temporal control, and factor coordination."

Abstract Deep Fusion Memory integrates predicted parametric memory into intermediate hidden states via gated cross-attention, reducing retrieval overhead and improving knowledge-intensive tasks without online retrieval.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models need effective ways to acquire and use external knowledge beyond what they retain from pretraining. Continued pretraining risks catastrophic forgetting, while retrieval at inference time can add substantial overhead. External parametric memory avoids online retrieval, but constructing its training data requires extensive offline search, and output-level interpolation provides only shallow fusion. We introduce Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), a parametric memory architecture that reduces this construction workload and incorporates learned memory into intermediate hidden states. A causal memory predictor learns to generate memory from the preceding context, using retrieved chunk embeddings from the corpus as supervision. The predicted memory is shared across tokens within each chunk and fused into their hidden states through gated cross-attention. Memory prediction and fusion are trained end to end with the backbone frozen, and inference requires no explicit retrieval. Compared with existing parametric memory approaches, which often construct token-level memory targets, DFM requires 32times fewer retrieval queries and 2048times fewer retrieved items to construct its training data. Across six QA tasks, DFM performs better over the strongest baseline by 12.71% on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and 11.19% on Qwen3-8B-Base. Beyond QA, domain adaptation yields average gains of 6.51 and 4.38 points over Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively.

КаUzzal каузальный предиктор памяти генерирует представление по предыдущему контексту, используя эмбеддинги извлечённых чанков как supervision, и память разделяется между токенами внутри чанка. Обучение memory predictor и fusion идёт end-to-end при замороженном backbone, а при инференсе явный retrieval не нужен, что снижает накладные расходы. По сравнению с аналогами DFM требует в 32 раза меньше запросов к retrieval и в 2048 раз меньше извлечённых элементов, при этом превосходит сильнейший baseline на QA-задачах (на 12.71% для Mistral-7B-v0.3 и 11.19% для Qwen3-8B-Base).", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Память, встроенная глубоко в модель, заменяет поиск при каждом запросе"}

Abstract Shared meta-experts across layers reduce redundancy and improve efficiency compared to layer-specific local experts in mixture-of-experts models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As model parameters scale up, sparsely activated Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches have become essential for model scaling. However, mainstream models currently adopt hierarchical architectures, where experts in different layers, although topologically independent, exhibit substantial similarity and redundancy in their stored content. Compared to the hierarchically arranged Local Expert in existing mainstream models, we hypothesize that inter-layer shared Meta Expert hold greater potential. To this end, we conduct a multi-dimensional empirical comparison between Local and Meta Experts, and find that models incorporating Meta Experts exhibit lower parameter redundancy, together with lower cross-entropy loss and higher downstream task accuracy. With the activated quantity and the expert granularity held fixed, our model matches the performance of Vanilla MoE using only 70--80\% of the experts, substantially improving the model's scaling potential. Beyond removing inter-layer redundancy among experts and improving information storage efficiency, our theoretical analysis further shows that, compared with Local Experts, Meta Experts provide hidden states with more flexible residual pathways, thereby improving information retrieval efficiency.

Авторы показывают, что у локальных экспертов в разных слоях сильно похожее содержимое, что приводит к избыточности параметров. Abstract LaLa-World is a language-conditioned latent world model that jointly learns from diverse simulation, robot, and video data to enable efficient model-predictive planning and policy initialization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small This paper shows that JEPA-style latent world models (LWMs) can learn across diverse domains and data sources by adopting the language-conditioned interface of video generation models. We establish this approach as an effective and efficient basis for planning with imagination and downstream policy fine-tuning. To this end, we introduce LaLa-World, a Language-conditioned Latent World model that learns from simulation and robot trajectories together with broader image and video data. Two designs make this work. First, we describe both numerical actions and visual changes in language, enabling joint training of a single future predictor across heterogeneous datasets. Second, we build the predictor on a language model, allowing it to interpret language conditions while predicting future latent states. The resulting predictor achieves higher average MPC planning performance than existing LWMs trained separately for each domain or task. Adding image and video data further improves average planning performance and robustness to action phrasing. In a matched comparison with an RAEv2-based video generation model baseline, LaLa-World reaches comparable planning performance with 6times less training cost and 13.9times faster planning. Beyond planning, the pretrained LaLa-World serves as an initialization for LaLa-Policy, which jointly predicts actions and future visual representations. LaLa-Policy performs competitively with Cosmos Policy on LIBERO and RoboCasa, with up to 5.5times faster inference.

Abstract A heartbeat-style speculative reasoning approach lets voice agents continue preparing tool calls during speech updates, improving coverage and reducing response latency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Tool-using voice agents often leave users waiting after speech ends, even when actionable information arrives earlier in the request. Speculative approaches begin work during speech, but chunk-bounded or interrupt-on-update generation can truncate a tool call before its arguments are complete. This couples computation time to speech updates, limiting useful work completed in advance. We introduce Heartbeat Thinking, which handles updated input through new Thinker invocations while allowing ongoing invocations and dispatched tools to finish. Periodic invocations read the latest partial transcript and a shared record of completed reasoning and tool results. These results help resolve missing arguments and initiate dependent calls, allowing preparation to progress across speech updates. Across SpeechFCEval, FDBench v3, and AudioCRAG, Heartbeat improves aggregate speculative tool coverage or retrieval reuse over a SHANKS-style windowed baseline, with benchmark-dependent effects on final task quality. On FDBench v3, it increases tool coverage by 16 percentage points and reduces mean final-response latency by 36.4\%. The results show that heartbeat thinking paradigm preserving ongoing computation during speech can turn listening time into reusable preparation for faster and more capable voice-agent responses.

Abstract LayerEditor unifies instruction-guided image editing with reusable RGBA layer generation via joint token modeling and edit-prior distillation, enabling precise local manipulation and layer reuse.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small High-quality image editing requires precise modification of target regions while preserving the content of unrelated regions. Existing editing models produce flattened outputs that limit precise local manipulation and layer reuse, while decomposition methods typically optimize layer recovery separately from instruction-driven editing. To this end, we introduce LayerEditor, a unified framework that couples instruction-guided image editing with controllable generation of reusable RGBA layers. At its core, In-Context RGB-Alpha Token Modeling jointly represents the edited canvas and an ordered sequence of RGB-Alpha layers within a single token sequence, enabling semantic edits and explicit control over individual layer content. To retain the pretrained model's editing capability during adaptation to layered outputs, we propose Compositional Edit-Prior Distillation, which transfers its editing prior to both the edited canvas and the image composed from the predicted layers. To support training and evaluation, we construct LayerEditor-100K, a large-scale paired image-editing dataset with ordered RGBA layer annotations, and introduce dedicated benchmarks for layered editing. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LayerEditor maintains strong editing fidelity while generating accurate RGBA layers, supporting both flexible layer-level manipulation and multi-layer decomposition.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract LayerEditor unifies instruction-guided image editing with reusable RGBA layer generation via joint token modeling and edit-prior distillation, enabling precise local manipulation and layer reuse.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small High-quality image editing requires precise modification of target regions while preserving the content of unrelated regions. Existing editing models produce flattened outputs that limit precise local manipulation and layer reuse, while decomposition methods typically optimize layer recovery separately from instruction-driven editing. To this end, we introduce LayerEditor, a unified framework that couples instruction-guided image editing with controllable generation of reusable RGBA layers. At its core, In-Context RGB-Alpha Token Modeling jointly represents the edited canvas and an ordered sequence of RGB-Alpha layers within a single token sequence, enabling semantic edits and explicit control over individual layer content. To retain the pretrained model's editing capability during adaptation to layered outputs, we propose Compositional Edit-Prior Distillation, which transfers its editing prior to both the edited canvas and the image composed from the predicted layers. To support training and evaluation, we construct LayerEditor-100K, a large-scale paired image-editing dataset with ordered RGBA layer annotations, and introduce dedicated benchmarks for layered editing. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LayerEditor maintains strong editing fidelity while generating accurate RGBA layers, supporting both flexible layer-level manipulation and multi-layer decomposition."

Abstract Code2Video-Compass is a benchmark evaluating multimodal language models on code-based video generation across text, image, and video inputs, revealing significant challenges in complex dynamic and state-dependent tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Code-based video generation offers fine-grained control, targeted editing, and component reuse compared with direct video generation. This approach requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to translate visual and temporal requirements into executable programs that produce complete videos. However, existing code-based benchmarks primarily target specialized tasks, leaving complete video creation across diverse creative categories and input modalities insufficiently evaluated. We introduce Code2Video-Compass, a general-purpose benchmark for code-based video generation across text-to-video (T2V), image-to-video (I2V), and video-to-video (V2V) settings. Code2Video-Compass comprises 2,104 tasks spanning 10 categories, 24 subtypes, and three complexity dimensions covering construction, dynamic animation, and state dependency. It combines tasks curated from textual instructions and visual references with corresponding T2V, I2V, and V2V tasks derived from complete professional and user-created videos. We propose a dual-level evaluation framework combining 12 objective metrics and five subjective metrics. Objective evaluation covers operational performance, video quality, text-video alignment, and reference fidelity, while subjective evaluation uses an MLLM judge to evaluate instruction following and holistic video quality. Extensive evaluation of 15 recent proprietary and open-weight MLLMs from nine families reveals that even leading models face challenges in reliably generating high-quality complete videos aligned with textual and visual requirements, with performance generally declining as construction, dynamic animation, and state-dependency complexity increases. We anticipate that Code2Video-Compass will advance reliable, coherent, and controllable code-based video generation and inspire broader progress in programmatic visual creation.
[23.10.2026 06:55] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Code2Video-Compass — универсальный бенчмарк для оценки мультимодальных LLM (MLLM), которые генерируют видео через написание исполняемого кода. Он охватывает три режима: text-to-video (T2V), image-to-video (I2V) и video-to-video (V2V), включает 2104 задачи в 10 категориях и 24 подтипах с учётом сложности построения, динамической анимации и зависимости от состояния. Оценка двухуровневая: 12 объективных метрик (производительность, качество видео, соответствие тексту и верность референсу) и 5 субъективных, где MLLM выступает в роли судьи. Abstract  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) describes a system that improves not only its behavior but also the process by which it improves. Recent digital agents test this idea by rewriting their own code and checking each version with executable tests. Bringing these mechanisms into the physical world requires learning from partial observations through costly trials, while accounting for changing operating conditions and potentially irreversible consequences. We position RSI within robot learning and trace pathways from improving robot capabilities to improving the processes that generate them. We examine how physical experience can strengthen these improvement mechanisms and when the resulting gains can support further self-improvement of the mechanisms themselves. Gains carry forward only when feedback addresses remaining learning needs, the receiving component can use the change, its value survives changes in tasks or hardware, and continued practice remains safe and affordable. Existing studies, concentrated in manipulation, support behavioral improvement, benefits to later learning, and procedure improvement. Some systems also change the improver's knowledge or tools, but the physical studies reviewed here have yet to isolate gains attributable to recursion. We synthesize these findings into evaluation principles and research directions for machines that, trial by trial, safely improve the way they improve.

Авторы анализируют, как опыт взаимодействия с физическим миром через дорогостоящие пробы может укреплять такие механизмы, и когда полученные выигрыши переносятся на последующее обучение. "Abstract  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) describes a system that improves not only its behavior but also the process by which it improves. Recent digital agents test this idea by rewriting their own code and checking each version with executable tests. Bringing these mechanisms into the physical world requires learning from partial observations through costly trials, while accounting for changing operating conditions and potentially irreversible consequences. We position RSI within robot learning and trace pathways from improving robot capabilities to improving the processes that generate them. We examine how physical experience can strengthen these improvement mechanisms and when the resulting gains can support further self-improvement of the mechanisms themselves. Gains carry forward only when feedback addresses remaining learning needs, the receiving component can use the change, its value survives changes in tasks or hardware, and continued practice remains safe and affordable. Existing studies, concentrated in manipulation, support behavioral improvement, benefits to later learning, and procedure improvement. Some systems also change the improver's knowledge or tools, but the physical studies reviewed here have yet to isolate gains attributable to recursion. We synthesize these findings into evaluation principles and research directions for machines that, trial by trial, safely improve the way they improve."

Abstract Verus-Insight improves AI-generated video detection by training artifact detectors through structured inspection planning, evidence verification, and evidence aggregation with human-anchored supervision.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in video generation have made synthetic videos increasingly realistic, placing greater demands on reliable AI-generated video detection. Existing artifact-based detectors typically identify generation artifacts as evidence for source prediction, yet they may either fail to recognize subtle artifacts or reach the correct source decision without faithfully identifying the evidence underlying it. In this work, we systematically investigate how to design and train effective artifact detectors and formulate artifact detection as a structured process comprising three complementary capabilities: Inspection Planning, which determines what should be examined; Evidence Verification, which verifies whether the inspected content exhibits genuine generation artifacts; and Evidence Aggregation, which integrates the verified evidence into the final source decision. Building on this formulation, we introduce Verus-Insight, trained with human-anchored full-process supervision and progressive capability training to strengthen both fine-grained artifact detection and video-level detection. We further introduce Verus-Bench, a benchmark with fine-grained human annotations of perceptible artifacts for evaluating artifact detection. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that Verus-Insight achieves strong performance in both fine-grained artifact analysis and AI-generated video detection, with substantial gains in recovering human-perceived artifacts. Further analyses reveal that the three capabilities are complementary yet distinct, and that Verus-Insight more reliably converts relevant inspection targets into supported artifact evidence without increasing spurious artifact claims.

Abstract RLSplat enables feed-forward 3D Gaussian splatting with linear reductions in latency, memory, and model size by allocating Gaussians regionally and decoding them via local attention.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (FF3DGS) reconstructs 3D scenes from images in a single forward pass, making it well-suited to applications that must operate within limited resource budgets. To further improve its practicality, recent budget-controllable FF3DGS methods allow the number of Gaussians to be adjusted at inference, so that the output meets a given storage limit or rendering-latency constraint. However, these methods overlook the cost of inference itself: to produce a representation with the target number of Gaussians, they first predict a dense set of Gaussians and then prune or select from it, requiring nearly constant latency and peak memory regardless of the budget. We therefore propose RLSplat, a resource-linear budget-controllable FF3DGS framework whose representation size, inference latency, and peak memory all decrease linearly as the Gaussian budget is reduced. Instead of pruning or selection, RLSplat learns a Gaussian allocation policy that distributes the budget across local scene regions, so that exactly the requested number of Gaussians is generated. These Gaussians are then refined through resource-linear Gaussian decoding, in which each Gaussian attends only to nearby Gaussians found via an efficient hierarchical neighbor search, so that the decoding cost decreases linearly with fewer Gaussians. In zero-shot evaluation, RLSplat outperforms prior budget-controllable methods at every budget, surpassing the strongest baseline even with 4times fewer Gaussians, while running up to 4.1times faster with 9.6times less peak memory at low budgets.
[23.10.2026 06:55] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен RLSplat — фреймворк для feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting, в котором число гауссиан задаётся заранее, а затраты на инференс линейно падают вместе с бюджетом. Вместо предсказания плотного набора гауссиан с последующим прунингом модель обучает policy распределения бюджета по локальным регионам сцены. Далее гауссианы уточняются через local attention с иерархическим поиском соседей, так что стоимость декодирования также растёт линейно. На zero-shot бенчмарках RLSplat превосходит предыдущие budget-controllable методы при любом бюджете, работая до 4.1 раза быстрее и с 9.6 раза меньшим пиковым потреблением памяти.", "emoji": "⚡", "title": "Столько гауссиан, сколько нужно: линейная экономия ресурсов в 3D-реконструкции"}

Abstract Test-time regression provides a learnable, closed-form alternative to pooling for compressing sequences in efficient language and multimodal models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Sequence compression is a fundamental operation and a critical information bottleneck in efficient language and multimodal models, and yet prevailing approaches based on pooling or learned projections remain underperforming. We revisit sequence compression through the lens of test-time regression: input tokens define a regression problem whose fitted coefficients form the compressed representation. Importantly, the regression problem is learned end-to-end via backpropagation, while its coefficients are computed efficiently in closed form during the forward pass. This leads to TTR-Compressor, a general alternative to conventional pooling and projection-based compression. We evaluate TTR-Compressor as the compressor for block-wise sparse attention and the visual token merger for multimodal large language models. On block-wise sparse attention, TTR-Compressor improves RULER performance over attention-based compressors by a large margin. For visual token merging, it consistently outperforms the standard MLP merger and matches its performance with 55.6% fewer visual tokens. These results establish test-time regression as an effective primitive for sequence compression in efficient language and multimodal models.
[23.10.2026 06:55] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается TTR-Compressor, новый способ сжатия последовательностей в эффективных LLM и мультимодальных моделях, основанный на test-time regression. Входные токены задают задачу регрессии, а её коэффициенты, вычисляемые в замкнутом виде в forward pass, и становятся сжатым представлением. Вся регрессия обучается end-to-end через backpropagation, что делает метод обучаемой альтернативой pooling и проекциям. На block-wise sparse attention он заметно улучшает результаты на RULER, а при объединении визуальных токенов в MLLM не уступает MLP-мержеру, используя на 55.6% меньше токенов.", "emoji": "🧮", "title": "Сжимаем последовательности через регрессию прямо во время инференса"}

Abstract Researchers introduce a benchmark and evaluation framework for video-centered deep research agents that synthesize external evidence into interactive reports linked to video moments.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Deep research agents synthesize open-web evidence into long reports, but current benchmarks do not evaluate research centered on a source video. We introduce Video-Centered Deep Research Report Generation. Given a video and user request, an agent identifies research questions, retrieves external multimodal evidence, and produces an interactive report linked to relevant video moments. This task has direct practical value because it helps users locate relevant moments, verify claims, reproduce demonstrated procedures, and make informed decisions from video content. We present a skill-based workflow and OmniDR-Bench for this task. For evaluation, we propose Omni Atomic Rubric Trees (Omni-ARTs). Each tree contains three dimensions, 23 shared directions, and task-specific atomic binary criteria. The directions test audiovisual grounding, multimodal evidence use, and interactive presentation. Audited Rubric Construction (ARC) builds these trees through independent evidence collection, automated checks, behavioral calibration, and iterative human review. OmniDR-Bench contains 50 Chinese and English tasks across 19 realworld subcategories. It includes 6,414 criteria. Across 18 agent configurations, the best system scores 63.3 out of 100. Current agents still need broader content coverage, stronger causal and logical analysis across modalities, and more accurate citation support. We further analyze models, harnesses, skill configurations, video categories, and research trajectories. The code and data are released at https://github.com/marinero4972/OmniDR-Bench.
[23.10.2026 06:55] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют новый бенчмарк OmniDR-Bench и систему оценки для задачи генерации исследовательских отчётов, где агент (deep research agent) опирается на исходное видео и подбирает внешние мультимодальные доказательства. Результатом становится интерактивный отчёт, ссылки в котором ведут на конкретные моменты видео. Для оценки предложены Omni-ARTs — деревья атомарных бинарных критериев, построенные процедурой ARC с независимым сбором данных, автоматическими проверками и итеративной разметкой людьми. Бенчмарк включает 50 задач на китайском и английском языках и 6414 критериев, а тестирование 18 конфигураций агентов показало лучший результат 63.3 балла из 100, что говорит о необходимости улучшения охвата контента, межмодального анализа и точности цитирования.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Агент, который изучает видео и пишет по нему исследование с ссылками на моменты"}

Abstract Raw retrieval-augmented generation over unprocessed conversation history outperforms complex agentic memory systems across multiple benchmarks, indicating that preserving raw evidence is more critical than elaborate memory transformations.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM memory is widely used in long-term conversation understanding. Agentic memory systems often use mutation, structural organization, learned retrieval strategies, or other complex operations. Is this complexity always necessary? We study Raw-RAG, a naive RAG baseline using dense retrieval and reranking over raw history to supply evidence to a reader LLM. Across LoCoMo, LongMemEval, MEME, and MemoryAgentBench, Raw-RAG ranks first among the compared memory systems in six of seven benchmark-model settings, outperforming the strongest compared agentic memory system by 0.13-5.53 points. We investigate why raw-history retrieval remains competitive. Evidence analysis shows that its higher coverage of necessary evidence can offset lower utilization. Useful evidence includes both answer values and the relations and dependency paths needed to derive them. Even complete access to transformed memory stores leaves an accuracy gap relative to raw evidence, highlighting the importance of preserving raw information. These findings challenge the necessity of complex agentic memory for the evaluated conversation tasks and establish naive RAG as an essential baseline.

Abstract Cola2 improves hierarchical language generation by jointly optimizing continuous semantic compression, reconstruction-generation balance, and encoder-decoder co-adaptation via dynamic span segmentation, masked diffusion decoding, and flow-matching priors.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Hierarchical language generation separates continuous semantic modeling from discrete text realization, but its effectiveness depends on what the latent space compresses and how the discrete generator uses it. We propose Cola2, a framework that jointly studies continuous semantic compression, the balance between reconstruction and generation, and continuous-discrete co-adaptation. Cola2 dynamically segments text into semantic spans, controls the compression rate through auxiliary-loss-free negative feedback, and combines reconstruction and generation supervision to learn compact, predictive latent representations. A block-causal masked diffusion decoder is trained with clean, noisy, and dropped latent conditions, while classifier-free guidance adjusts the balance between semantic reconstruction and autonomous generation. Token-prediction gradients update the encoder through the latents, whereas Flow Matching prior learning is decoupled from representation learning by stop-gradient. During inference, generated text is re-encoded under a fixed span layout to keep the latent history consistent with the actual text context. Experiments on five downstream tasks with about 210 billion training tokens validate the core designs and show competitive, unsaturated scaling. With 346.03 billion source tokens, an unconverged checkpoint reaches a 60.10 average across eight tasks and surpasses Llama-3-8B on HellaSwag and StoryCloze despite its reported pretraining budget being over 43 times larger. These results demonstrate the potential of continuous-latent hierarchical language generation.

Модель динамически разбивает текст на смысловые спаны и совместно оптимизирует сжатие латентного пространства, баланс между реконструкцией и генерацией, а также адаптацию энкодера и декодера. Декодер на основе block-causal masked diffusion обучается с чистыми, зашумлёнными и отброшенными латентными условиями, а Flow Matching prior обучается отдельно через stop-gradient. Несмотря на меньший бюджет предобучения, непредобученная версия модели с 346 млрд токенов превосходит Llama-3-8B на HellaSwag и StoryCloze.", "emoji": "🧩", "title": "Сжимать смысл, а не слова: иерархическая генерация текста в непрерывном латентном пространстве"}

Abstract AgentVQA is an offline benchmark assessing vision-language models on realistic agentic tasks across web, robotics, video, games, and spatial planning, revealing that visual grounding and state tracking errors are the primary failure modes.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Vision-language models (VLMs) can perform a broad range of tasks across diverse settings, yet their performance in agentic contexts remains poorly understood. Although online evaluations in simulators and real-world environments are essential for measuring interactive agent performance, they are costly and challenging to scale. Complementary offline VQA benchmarks can enable faster and more reliable comparisons across agentic domains, yet existing benchmarks remain limited and fragmented. To address this gap, we introduce AgentVQA, an offline benchmark for evaluating VLMs on realistic agentic scenarios across five agent domains: Web Agents, Robotics, Egocentric Videos, Games, and Spatial Planning. We build AgentVQA by manually selecting 1000 challenging, real-world agent examples from 23 source datasets and adapting them for reliable offline evaluation. The resulting benchmark preserves realistic scenarios from interactive environments across diverse domains, and its model rankings track those from corresponding online evaluations. Across the 22 evaluated models, the best achieves only 58.5% accuracy. Our cross-domain analysis shows that errors are driven primarily by failures to ground and maintain accurate representations of the current and evolving visual state, including localizing relevant UI elements, interpreting motion, tracking state changes over time, and inferring 3D spatial relationships, rather than by task misinterpretation or downstream logical reasoning.
[23.10.2026 06:56] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен AgentVQA — оффлайн-бенчмарк для оценки vision-language моделей (VLM) на реалистичных агентных задачах в пяти доменах: веб-агенты, робототехника, egocentric-видео, игры и пространственное планирование. Авторы вручную отобрали 1000 сложных примеров из 23 датасетов, адаптировав их для надёжной оффлайн-оценки, при этом рейтинги моделей совпадают с онлайн-оценками в симуляторах. Лучшая из 22 протестированных моделей достигает лишь 58.5% точности. Анализ ошибок показывает, что главные причины — неспособность к visual grounding и отслеживанию состояния визуальной сцены, а не неверное понимание задачи или логические рассуждения.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "VLM-агентам не хватает зрения: ошибки в понимании состояния сцены, а не в логике"}

Complementary offline VQA benchmarks can enable faster and more reliable comparisons across agentic domains, yet existing benchmarks remain limited and fragmented. To address this gap, we introduce AgentVQA, an offline benchmark for evaluating VLMs on realistic agentic scenarios across five agent domains: Web Agents, Robotics, Egocentric Videos, Games, and Spatial Planning. We build AgentVQA by manually selecting 1000 challenging, real-world agent examples from 23 source datasets and adapting them for reliable offline evaluation. The resulting benchmark preserves realistic scenarios from interactive environments across diverse domains, and its model rankings track those from corresponding online evaluations. Across the 22 evaluated models, the best achieves only 58.5% accuracy. Our cross-domain analysis shows that errors are driven primarily by failures to ground and maintain accurate representations of the current and evolving visual state, including localizing relevant UI elements, interpreting motion, tracking state changes over time, and inferring 3D spatial relationships, rather than by task misinterpretation or downstream logical reasoning."

Abstract This survey unifies video understanding and generation through world modeling, organizing progress around inferring, synthesizing, sharing, and interacting with latent temporal states to emphasize persistent dynamics over clip-level perception.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Video foundation models have advanced rapidly in understanding and generating visual sequences, while video world models seek to capture the underlying dynamics of the world they depict. Despite their different objectives, both concern the relationship between video observations and an evolving world. This survey adopts world modeling as a unifying lens for examining these developments. Viewing an observed video as a partial, lossy, and temporally ordered projection of a latent temporal world, we organize the literature around four complementary operations: reading infers world properties from observed video; writing synthesizes video from specified or learned world conditions; sharing integrates understanding and generation through common modeling mechanisms; and interacting predicts and updates world states under actions and evolving observations. This perspective distinguishes temporal coherence in observed or generated sequences from consistency in the underlying world state. Across tasks, representations, models, datasets, and benchmarks, we trace a shift in emphasis from clip-level recognition and perceptual fidelity toward persistent state, controllable evolution, physical and causal validity, and evaluation grounded in interaction and downstream decisions, and we distill the open challenges that follow.

Авторы выделяют четыре операции: чтение (inference свойств мира из видео), запись (синтез видео по условиям мира), совместное использование общих механизмов и взаимодействие, при котором предсказываются и обновляются состояния мира под действиями. Abstract RouteWeaver unifies heterogeneous LLM routing modes via coordinated training and progressive exploration, improving accuracy across diverse reasoning tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM routing dynamically selects and coordinates heterogeneous language models across diverse tasks. However, existing approaches are largely built around a predefined execution paradigm, such as single-round, multi-round, or agentic routing, which fixes how model calls are organized and limits adaptation to different query needs. We propose RouteWeaver, a unified routing framework that integrates these routing modes within a single LLM policy, jointly learning which mode to use and how to select models within that mode. To jointly optimize mode selection and within-mode execution, we develop COMET-GRPO (COordinated Mode-Execution Training), which decouples the trajectory-level advantage into separate signals for mode selection and within-mode execution, enabling more targeted credit assignment to each decision level. We further introduce progressive exploration, which gradually shifts training from externally assigned modes to autonomous selection, so that the router first learns how to execute each mode before deciding when to use it. We evaluate RouteWeaver across mathematics, coding, knowledge, logical reasoning, and factual recall. RouteWeaver achieves an average accuracy of 61.4%, an 11.03% relative improvement over the strongest baseline, and outperforms all compared baselines in each of the five domains. Ablations show that both COMET-GRPO and progressive exploration are important for adaptive routing; without progressive exploration, the routing policy collapses entirely to single-round execution.
[23.10.2026 06:56] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен RouteWeaver — единая система маршрутизации, которая объединяет разные режимы работы LLM (одношаговый, многошаговый и агентный) в одной политике. Для совместной оптимизации выбора режима и выбора моделей внутри него авторы предлагают COMET-GRPO, которая разделяет advantage траектории на два отдельных сигнала. Прогрессивное исследование постепенно переводит обучение от заранее заданных режимов к автономному выбору, что помогает модели сначала освоить каждый режим. На тестах по математике, программированию, знаниям, логике и фактам RouteWeaver достигает средней точности 61.4%, что на 11.03% выше сильнейшего базового метода.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Один маршрутизатор, все режимы: адаптивный выбор LLM под каждую задачу"}

Abstract MolLean introduces a verifiable chemical reasoning framework with explicit molecular states, Lean-based verification, and interactive tool feedback to reduce hallucinations across generation and reaction tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Chemical chain-of-thought can contain hallucinated structural and property claims. We thereby introduce MolLean, a process-verifiable language that grounds chemical reasoning in an explicit molecular state. A layered Lean-based kernel checks state transitions and assertions over molecular readouts, from primitive graph edits to compound transformations. Meanwhile, a typed witness protocol separates native structural checks from externally supplied measurements. We further introduce MolLean-MCP, a stateful tool interface that exposes these transitions, checks, and readouts during generation: the model receives feedback to decide its next action, and rejected actions leave the state unchanged. MolLean supports one-pass trace execution, whole-trace refinement, and interactive reasoning through MolLean-MCP. We evaluate these three modes with direct chain-of-thought on 8,800 matched examples across 12 tasks spanning molecular generation, editing, optimization, and reactions. With Qwen3.6-27B, whole-trace refinement outperforms direct chain-of-thought on all 12 tasks. MolLean-MCP complements whole-trace refinement with stepwise verification feedback, reaching better performance in reaction tasks. Together, these results highlight an executable foundation for chemical reasoning, in which explicit chemical commitments can be checked and revised rather than remaining solely in free-form explanations.

Abstract Policy-Mask learns a budget-conditioned selection policy over diffusion timesteps, network blocks, and channels to extract compact, task-optimal features for discriminative vision tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Selecting intermediate features from a frozen diffusion model for discriminative tasks is non-trivial because the useful signal is distributed across the denoising trajectory, network depth, and channels. In practice, this requires choosing a timestep, a block, and a channel subset. Existing methods either fix these choices by hand or optimize only a few extraction hyperparameters, most often the timestep, then reuse the same rule across inputs and budgets. We introduce Policy-Mask, a budget-conditioned policy trained on the downstream objective that jointly selects all three under a prescribed channel budget. Without normalization, the channel-score contribution grows with the number of selected channels and dominates the timestep and block updates. We normalize it by subset cardinality, keeping the three policy heads on comparable scales and stabilizing joint training. At matched budgets, Policy-Mask improves over fixed extraction and feature-filter baselines across segmentation, classification, and semantic correspondence, reaching 38.9 mIoU on ADE20K, 75.4% top-1 on CIFAR-100 at 128 dimensions, and 76.3 PCK@0.1 on SPair-71k. Component-wise ablations show that block selection materially affects downstream accuracy, and the highest recorded channel-budget score occurs at 256 retained channels, identifying a practical operating point for compact diffusion representations. We further adapt Policy-Mask to diffusion transformers, including PixArt-Σ and SD3.5 MMDiT, by replacing U-Net channels with image-token hidden dimensions.

Ключевая проблема в том, что полезный сигнал распределён по траектории денойзинга, глубине сети и каналам, а прежние методы фиксируют эти решения вручную или оптимизируют только timestep. Политика обучается по целевой задаче downstream, а нормализация вклада каналов по мощности подмножества стабилизирует совместное обучение трёх голов. Метод превосходит фиксированные и фильтрующие базовые подходы в сегментации, классификации и семантическом соответствии, а также адаптирован к диффузионным трансформерам вроде PixArt-Σ и SD3.5 MMDiT.", "emoji": "🎯", "title": "Умный выбор признаков из диффузии под заданный бюджет"}

Abstract Reinforcement learning improves reasoning accuracy without requiring a specific wait marker, which originates from training data rather than RL, and automated evaluation metrics can misrepresent reasoning trends.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Reinforcement learning (RL) is credited with teaching reasoning models to write {wait} before reconsidering a step, yet large RL accuracy gains do not require the word. This view can confuse what RL discovers with what a model inherits from its training data. We trace the word through open models' training histories, reading it with label-free indices of frequency, position, form and usage and an injected-error test, all computed from sampled outputs. We find that zero-RL almost never coins the marker: on bases that lack the word, RL raises MATH-500 accuracy substantially while {wait} stays near absent. It arrives with training data: mid-training in OLMo-3, and fine-tuning on reasoning traces installs it in most models whose base lacks it. Where the base already uses it, both released RL-Zero recipes (OLMo-3, MiMo) multiply it, whereas KL-free GRPO from the same base does not; OLMo-3's recipe wears down its form and makes it a more sensitive but more liberal alarm; RL after supervised fine-tuning barely changes it, and our GRPO recipe erases a marker seeded by fine-tuning. Finally, we show that marker frequency alone does not indicate RL-induced reasoning and caution against LLM-as-a-judge revision metrics, as automated judges can reverse human-labelled trends.

"Abstract Reinforcement learning improves reasoning accuracy without requiring a specific wait marker, which originates from training data rather than RL, and automated evaluation metrics can misrepresent reasoning trends.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Reinforcement learning (RL) is credited with teaching reasoning models to write {wait} before reconsidering a step, yet large RL accuracy gains do not require the word. We trace the word through open models' training histories, reading it with label-free indices of frequency, position, form and usage and an injected-error test, all computed from sampled outputs. Finally, we show that marker frequency alone does not indicate RL-induced reasoning and caution against LLM-as-a-judge revision metrics, as automated judges can reverse human-labelled trends."

Abstract AnyGameRenderer is a video-to-video generative model that transforms low-quality agent-built game footage into high-fidelity visuals while preserving layout, camera, and entity timing.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small AI coding agents can now build small playable games in minutes, but the results usually look like programmer art: gray blockouts, colored geometric primitives, and flat semantic blobs. We present AnyGameRenderer, a video-to-video generative renderer that turns such footage into high-quality visuals with characters, motion detail, and effects. Throughout, it keeps the layout, camera, and every entity's position and timing intact.   Our AnyGameRenderer generalizes across a wide range of genres that agents build, such as tower defense, auto-battlers, top-down survivors, turn-based battles, kart racing, and crowd runners.   AnyGameRenderer takes plain video as its game-state input, with no complex representations such as engine G-buffers, per-object class labels, or low-poly proxies. Gray blockouts, colored primitives, semantic maps, and wireframes all work as input.   It also preserves entity correspondence: group counts stay correct, and entities appear and disappear on the right frame.   We implement AnyGameRenderer by LoRA fine-tuning MiniMax-H3 ref2va, a 33B reference-video-conditioned video generation model. Training uses sim10k paired clips from three sources: (i) multi-stream rendering data from 38 agent-made games exported from GEAR, an AI game-creation tool; (ii) two-stream rendering data from Unreal Engine, with programmatically controlled crowds, count changes, and entity scales; and (iii) real gameplay recordings, with pairs reconstructed via 3D-box detection.   To evaluate the entity correspondence, we propose an entity-level benchmark of 59 held-out cases with 753 entities, scored against ground-truth boxes. Compared with the base model, AnyGameRenderer better preserves entity correspondence: temporal match rises from 50.9\% to 62.9\%, missed count changes fall from 34.7\% to 21.3\%, and fewer entities are hallucinated or left unrendered.

Модель сохраняет раскладку, камеру и позиции с таймингом всех сущностей, принимая на вход даже серые блокауты или wireframe. Она дообучена через LoRA на базе 33B-модели MiniMax-H3 ref2va на парных данных из игр, Unreal Engine и реальных записей геймплея. Оценка по собственному entity-level benchmark показывает рост temporal match с 50.9% до 62.9% и снижение пропущенных изменений количества сущностей.", "emoji": "🎮", "title": "Из серых кубиков — в красивую игру, не теряя ни одного персонажа"}

Abstract SCOPE compiles compound policies into optimized neural-symbolic programs that achieve high accuracy, stability, and interpretability on inexpensive models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale.
[23.10.2026 06:57] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен SCOPE — метод, который компилирует составную политику безопасности в исполняемую нейросимволическую программу из нейронных предикатов, детерминированных экстракторов и кода, объединяющего их выходы. Программа оптимизируется совместно с обучающими данными, а для поиска слабых мест используется состязательная генерация контрпримеров, где программа расходится с политикой. На реальных задачах SCOPE показывает наивысшую среднюю точность среди методов оптимизации промптов, декомпозиции задач и генерации воркфлоу. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract SCOPE compiles compound policies into optimized neural-symbolic programs that achieve high accuracy, stability, and interpretability on inexpensive models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale."

Abstract Optimized unmasking policies for masked diffusion language models improve efficiency by using trajectory-contraction targets with confidence and hidden states, achieving better speed-accuracy trade-offs with minimal overhead.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Masked diffusion language models generate tokens in parallel, but their efficiency depends on which predictions are committed. Learned unmasking policies improve the speed--accuracy trade-off, yet differ in supervision, inputs, and capacity, obscuring which choices matter. We view supervised policies as distilling a decoding procedure through a teacher and examine independently what the teacher labels as committable, on which states the policy imitates it, what the policy observes, and how much the number of function evaluations (NFE) must decrease to offset policy overhead. We find that labeling predictions matching the final output is overly permissive at low NFE, and hidden states are weak alone but can complement confidence. About a 10% NFE reduction suffices to offset policy overhead for policies up to 7.5M parameters across tested settings. We therefore train a policy on trajectory-contraction targets, which accept only commitments staying on a reference trajectory, using confidence and hidden states. On LLaDA-8B-Instruct, the resulting 856K-parameter policy achieves the best accuracy--NFE frontier and highest throughput on GSM8K, is competitive at moderate targets on MATH-500 and HumanEval, and largely transfers to Dream-7B-Instruct.
[23.10.2026 06:57] Response: {"desc": "Статья посвящена оптимизации порядка декодирования в masked diffusion языковых моделях, где токены генерируются параллельно, а эффективность зависит от того, какие предсказания фиксируются на каждом шаге. Авторы рассматривают обученную политику разметки (unmasking policy) как дистилляцию процедуры декодирования учителя и разделяют её компоненты: правила разметки, состояния, которые она имитирует, входные признаки и ёмкость модели. Выяснилось, что простое совпадение предсказаний с финальным ответом слишком мягко при малом числе вычислений, а скрытые состояния слабы сами по себе, но дополняют уверенность модели. Abstract TILDE distills embodied foundation model capabilities into robot policies via on-policy teacher supervision and autonomous recovery data collection, improving real-world task performance without large-scale action pretraining.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large-scale action pretraining equips Embodied Foundation Models (EFMs) with strong manipulation capabilities, with particular strengths in generalization and closed-loop robustness, but reproducing such pretraining requires substantial robot data and computation. To transfer these capabilities from pretrained EFMs to policies without action pretraining, we introduce TILDE (Teacher-In-the-Loop Distillation from EFMs), which synergizes on-policy distillation at student-induced states with learning from teacher-executed recovery trajectories. We further propose AutoDAgger, an autonomous data aggregation pipeline in which a robotic reward model is utilized to monitor task progress and trigger teacher intervention, thereby collecting recovery experience without human monitoring or manual control. AutoDAgger accelerates recovery-trajectory collection by more than 2times compared with the human-in-the-loop method. By unifying learning from demonstrations, on-policy teacher supervision, and teacher's corrective behavior, TILDE improves task performance by up to 4.1times over behavior cloning across three real-world tasks and supports effective cross-architecture distillation.
[23.10.2026 06:57] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен метод TILDE, который переносит возможности предобученных Embodied Foundation Models (EFM) в политики роботов без дорогостоящего предобучения на действиях. Ключевая идея сочетает on-policy дистилляцию на состояниях, порождённых самим студентом, с обучением на траекториях восстановления, выполненных учителем. Для сбора таких траекторий предложен AutoDAgger, в котором робототехническая reward model автоматически отслеживает прогресс задачи и запускает вмешательство учителя без участия человека. Abstract F2G-Pose is a real-time feed-forward framework that predicts category-level object pose, metric size, and completed shape from a single RGB-D image using dense visual features, DGCNN tokenization, and a Mixture-of-Experts Transformer.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small This paper studies category-level RGB-D object pose estimation, which recovers an object's 3D rotation and translation without instance-specific CAD models or reference views. This task is highly ambiguous due to intra-class variations, symmetries, and partial observations. Current solutions struggle to balance reliability and efficiency: methods based on intermediate structures are prone to error propagation, while diffusion models suffer from high inference latency and complex post-hoc candidate extraction. To address this, we present F2G-Pose, a real-time direct, single-pass framework that predicts category-level pose, metric size, and camera-frame completed shape from a single RGB-D observation in one feed-forward pass. F2G-Pose lifts dense visual foundation features to partial point clouds, encodes local geometry with DGCNN tokenization, and integrates object-level structure with a Mixture-of-Experts Transformer. Joint size prediction and camera-frame shape completion encourage metric consistency through dense geometric supervision. With its task-specific network trained only on pose-annotated synthetic SOPE data and its frozen RADIO backbone externally pretrained, F2G-Pose achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on SOPE and ROPE while running at 28 FPS for pose-only inference, and further demonstrates strong camera-frame geometry on HouseCat6D and real-object transfer on HANDAL. Analyses of latency, robustness, shape completion, and GenPose++ candidate behavior further demonstrate the efficiency and reliability of F2G-Pose, with real-world manipulation experiments demonstrating deployment potential under segmented RGB-D inputs. The project page, code, and pretrained models are made available at https://jrryzh.github.io/F2G-Pose/.

Модель предсказывает 3D-вращение и трансляцию, метрический размер и полную форму объекта в системе координат камеры без использования CAD-моделей. Abstract FRED improves single-step future video representations in world action models through self-distillation and temporal conditioning, boosting robot policy performance on long-horizon and unseen tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising approach to learning generalist robot policies using rich spatiotemporal priors from pre-trained video diffusion models. A recent line of WAMs improves inference efficiency by conditioning a separate action decoder on intermediate future video representations extracted from a single denoising step, avoiding costly iterative denoising required for video generation. This design makes action prediction depend directly on the quality of these future representations. In this paper, we introduce Future REpresentation learning via self-Distillation with temporal context (FRED), a framework that trains WAMs to improve the quality of future video representations from noise in a single denoising step. Specifically, FRED uses self-distillation to predict future representations from noise, using representations extracted from ground-truth future observations as targets. We further condition the video model on historical observations, providing temporal context for predicting future representations better. Across diverse real-world manipulation scenarios, FRED consistently outperforms competing WAMs. It achieves particularly large gains on long-horizon and out-of-domain tasks under zero-shot environment generalization, and substantially improves human-to-robot skill transfer, reaching 50.0% task success on tasks absent from robot training data and demonstrated only in human videos, compared with 19.4% for the best competing WAM.
